<!--
  Hand-authored anatomy concepts for 104 CPS — Cardiopulmonary System, Year 1,
  Kasr Al Ainy (kau). This file is the anatomy-department companion to
  ../concept/104-CPS-concepts.md (GENERATED by scripts/kasr/build-batches.ts —
  never edited by hand) and to ../concept/104-CPS-practical-concepts.md
  (histology practical stations). Anatomy department book, all 14 chapters,
  was almost entirely missing before this file: the generated batch above
  carries only 6 anatomy concepts, one or two per chapter, drawn from a single
  end-of-year written paper. This file covers the other 9 chapters that had
  no concept at all, and adds depth to the 5 chapters the generated batch
  already touched, so every one of the book's 14 chapters is now covered.

  Source: `Dpt book anatomy 2026 104.pdf`, manifest source ID
  src_4bd55e9eaf092282818c, 157 pages, native text. Printed page = page index
  + 1 (index 0 is printed page 1). Page text is cached at
  scripts/kasr/extract/pagetext/src_4bd55e9eaf092282818c.json; the full book
  was read from that committed cache chapter by chapter — no extractor was
  re-run to write this file.

  66 new concepts. Every one was checked against live state and every pending
  batch with `tools/find-existing.mjs` before minting (shortest distinctive
  word first, not the full label). Several genuine near-misses were found —
  the live library already carries a large set of very atomic, single-fact
  CVS/RES concepts (valve surface projections, individual coronary-vessel
  facts, individual lung-groove facts) that are not module-tagged to 104 CPS,
  and the pending `docs/import-ready/concept/SYS-CVS-CONCEPT-T08.md` batch
  already covers atrial septal defect, ventricular septal defect, tetralogy of
  Fallot, patent ductus arteriosus and coarctation of the aorta as clinical
  entities. Where a planned concept would have duplicated one of these, it was
  either dropped, rescoped narrower, or replaced with book content those
  batches do not cover (three rarer aortic-arch anomalies in place of a
  second PDA/coarctation concept). Every near-miss actually found is recorded
  on the record it was checked against, in `rejected_merge_candidate_ids`
  field_notes, so the next author does not have to re-decide it.

  IDs are minted with `scripts/kasr/seeds/types.ts:mintConceptId("104 CPS",
  subject, canonical_key)` — the Kasr pipeline mint, not the generic manual's
  `tools/mint-concept-id.mjs` — and checked for collision against both live
  state and every file under docs/Kasr-Source-Imports/concept/.

  Subject placement: `cvs` for heart, pericardium and the thoracic vessels;
  `resp` for lungs, pleura, diaphragm, thoracic cage/wall and the tubes
  proper to breathing; `dev` for both development chapters (primary_node_id
  DIS-EMB-T03 for mechanism, DIS-EMB-T04 for anomaly, matching the existing
  aortic-sac-fate concept's own placement); `gi` for the oesophagus, a GI
  tube passing through the thorax; `haem` for the thoracic duct, right
  lymphatic duct and thoracic lymph nodes, matching this module's own
  histology practical batch's placement of lymphoid content under `haem`.
  `msk` for the thoracic cage, per the lane brief's own suggestion — the
  chapter is pure bone and cartilage and the taxonomy's MSK regional-anatomy
  tree has no closer fit. All primary placement is `DIS-ANA-T04` (Anatomy >
  Thorax), matching the generated batch's own 6 concepts, except the two
  development-chapter concepts noted above.

  Scope note on `atomic_claim_ids` and evidence: this lane's file ownership
  was scoped to concept and article records only (this file and
  ../article/104-CPS-anatomy.md) — authoring the evidence chain (claim,
  citation and span records in ../evidence/) was out of scope this round.
  `atomic_claim_ids` is therefore `[clear]` on every new concept here, which
  `medical:audit` will report as a missing required field for each of these
  66 IDs until an evidence-authoring pass runs against them. This is a known,
  structural gap from the file-ownership split, not a sign the content is
  unsourced — every definition below carries a page-cited `original_wording`
  quote from the department book, and `resource_ids` names the book on every
  record.

  Import: Admin › Concepts › Import.

# Item
## label
The thoracic cage is a conical osteocartilaginous framework, narrower above than below, with an inlet, a cavity and an outlet

## id
CON-MSK-54A6BFC89A697C

## canonical_key
thoracic-cage.composition-and-function

## definition
The thoracic cage is the osteocartilaginous skeleton of the thorax: sternum and costal cartilages in front, thoracic vertebrae, intervertebral discs and the shafts of the twelve pairs of ribs behind, and the ribs on each side. It is conical, with the anterior wall shorter than the posterior wall. The cavity it encloses has a narrow inlet continuous with the root of the neck, a cavity holding the lungs and heart, and a broad outlet closed by the diaphragm.

## explicit_objective
State the three components that form the thoracic cage and the three parts of the cavity it encloses.

## pitfalls
Describing the cage as symmetrical front to back. The book states the anterior wall is shorter than the posterior wall, which is why the inlet and outlet planes both slope rather than sitting horizontal.

# Item
## label
The thoracic inlet is bounded by the manubrium in front, the first thoracic vertebra behind and the first ribs on each side

## id
CON-MSK-536842A4CEBF76

## canonical_key
thoracic-inlet.boundaries

## definition
The thoracic inlet is the narrow upper opening that connects the thoracic cavity with the root of the neck. Its plane is oblique, sloping downwards and forwards. It is bounded by the upper border of the manubrium sterni in front, the body of the first thoracic vertebra behind, and the inner border of the first rib on each side.

## explicit_objective
Name the three boundaries of the thoracic inlet and describe the slope of its plane.

## pitfalls
Confusing the inlet's slope with the outlet's. The inlet slopes downwards and forwards; the outlet slopes downwards and backwards — a detail examiners test by asking for both in the same question.

# Item
## label
The thoracic outlet is bounded by the xiphoid process in front, T12 and the eleventh and twelfth ribs behind, and the costal cartilages of the seventh to tenth ribs on each side

## id
CON-MSK-6E1B99AFD7AA43

## canonical_key
thoracic-outlet.boundaries

## definition
The thoracic outlet is the broad lower opening of the thoracic cage, closed by the diaphragm and sloping downwards and backwards. It is bounded by the xiphoid process in front, the body of the twelfth thoracic vertebra and the eleventh and twelfth pairs of ribs behind, and the costal cartilages of the seventh, eighth, ninth and tenth ribs on each side.

## explicit_objective
Name the boundaries of the thoracic outlet and state what closes it.

## pitfalls
Naming the diaphragm as a boundary rather than as the structure that closes the outlet. The book lists three boundaries — front, back, sides — and separately says the outlet 'is closed by the diaphragm'.

# Item
## label
The intercostal space has three muscle layers — external, internal and innermost — of which the external is the main inspiratory elevator of the rib

## id
CON-RES-671ABF9D60BE19

## canonical_key
intercostal-muscles.layers-and-action

## definition
Each intercostal space holds three layers of muscle from superficial to deep: the external intercostal, running obliquely downwards and forwards from the tubercle of the rib above to the costochondral junction, where it becomes the anterior intercostal membrane; the internal intercostal, running obliquely downwards and backwards from the sternum to the angle of the rib, continuing as the posterior intercostal membrane; and the innermost intercostal, in the same plane and direction as the internal but separated from it by the neurovascular bundle. All three are supplied by the corresponding intercostal nerve. The external layer elevates the ribs and is inspiratory; some authors restrict elevation to the external layer alone and treat the internal and innermost as depressors, though both layers also brace the space against being sucked in on inspiration or bulged out on expiration.

## explicit_objective
Name the three intercostal muscle layers in order from superficial to deep, state the direction of their fibres, and give the action of the external layer.

## pitfalls
Giving all three layers the same action. The book attributes elevation (inspiration) mainly to the external layer, while some authors treat the internal and innermost as expiratory depressors — and all three share the separate job of bracing the space against paradoxical movement.

# Item
## label
A typical intercostal nerve runs in the neurovascular bundle below the vein and artery, and ends as the anterior cutaneous branch

## id
CON-RES-0BB6BDDB3E4413

## canonical_key
typical-intercostal-nerve.course-and-branches

## definition
The typical intercostal nerves are the anterior primary rami of the second to sixth thoracic spinal nerves; they run their whole course in the thoracic wall. Posteriorly each nerve lies between the parietal pleura and the posterior intercostal membrane; in the costal groove it runs in the neurovascular bundle below the vein and artery, between the internal and innermost intercostal muscles; anteriorly it crosses in front of the internal thoracic vessels before piercing the wall as the anterior cutaneous branch. Its branches are ganglionic rami communicantes to the sympathetic chain, a collateral branch to the muscles, muscular branches, a lateral cutaneous branch, the terminal anterior cutaneous branch, articular branches and pleural branches. The second nerve's lateral cutaneous branch crosses the axilla to the arm as the intercostobrachial nerve instead of dividing locally.

## explicit_objective
Trace a typical intercostal nerve from the intervertebral foramen to the anterior cutaneous branch, naming its position relative to the vein and artery at each part of its course.

## pitfalls
Forgetting that the nerve is the lowest of the three neurovascular structures (VAN — vein, artery, nerve, from above down) in the costal groove, which is why a needle placed against the lower border of the rib above is safest.

mechanism

# Item
## label
The internal thoracic artery descends behind the upper six costal cartilages to end opposite the sixth space as the musculophrenic and superior epigastric arteries

## id
CON-CVS-1861B888CFF02D

## canonical_key
internal-thoracic-artery.origin-course-and-branches

## definition
The internal thoracic (internal mammary) artery arises from the first part of the subclavian artery in the root of the neck, one inch above the sternal end of the clavicle. It descends behind the clavicle and the upper six costal cartilages, half an inch from the lateral border of the sternum, in front of the parietal pleura and lung apex, and ends opposite the sixth intercostal space by dividing into the superior epigastric and musculophrenic arteries. Along its course it gives the pericardiacophrenic artery, six pairs of anterior intercostal branches, perforating branches to the skin and medial mammary gland, and pericardial, sternal and mediastinal branches.

## explicit_objective
State the origin, course and two terminal branches of the internal thoracic artery, and name the branch that supplies the pericardium and diaphragm alongside the phrenic nerve.

## pitfalls
Forgetting that the perforating branches of the second, third and fourth spaces must be ligated in radical mastectomy — if left, they retract and cause internal haemorrhage, which is the clinical reason this artery's branches matter beyond the thorax.

# Item
## label
Below the sternal angle the thoracic wall is supplied segmentally by intercostal dermatomes starting at T2, with the missing C5 to T1 band carried away by the upper limb

## id
CON-RES-A774BB8400B802

## canonical_key
dermatomes-of-thoracic-wall.cutaneous-innervation

## definition
Skin of the thoracic wall above the sternal angle is supplied by C3 and C4 through the supraclavicular nerves; below that level it is supplied by the thoracic nerves, starting at T2, through the lateral and anterior cutaneous branches of the intercostal nerves. Each intercostal nerve supplies an oblique band of skin one segment above the vertebral level it corresponds to — the skin over the xiphoid process, for example, is T6 although it lies opposite the T9 vertebra. The segments C5 to T1, missing from this sequence, were drawn out with the developing limb bud to supply the upper limb as the brachial plexus. The lower five intercostal nerves and the subcostal nerve continue their bands onto the abdominal wall, and the posterior thoracic wall is supplied by the cutaneous branches of the posterior primary rami.

## explicit_objective
State which spinal segments supply the thoracic wall above and below the sternal angle, and explain why C5 to T1 are absent from the sequence on the trunk.

## pitfalls
Reading the dermatome level as matching the vertebral level behind it. The book's own example — T6 skin lying opposite the T9 vertebra — shows the two are offset, because the dermatome band follows the nerve's root value, not the rib it happens to lie beside.

# Item
## label
The parietal pleura is named cervical, costal, mediastinal and diaphragmatic by the wall it lines, and each pleural sac has a costomediastinal and a costodiaphragmatic recess

## id
CON-RES-CED26421AF8B39

## canonical_key
pleura.divisions-and-recesses

## definition
Each pleural sac is a closed serous sac invaginated from its medial side by the lung, forming a visceral layer adherent to the lung and a parietal layer, with the pleural cavity between them holding only a thin film of fluid. The parietal pleura is subdivided by the wall it lines: cervical pleura over the lung apex, costal pleura against the ribs and intercostal spaces, mediastinal pleura against the sides of the mediastinum — which encloses the root of the lung and continues below it as the pulmonary ligament, a loose fold that gives the pulmonary veins room to distend — and diaphragmatic pleura over the diaphragm. Two recesses in each sac are not occupied by lung except in forced inspiration: the costomediastinal recess, between costal and mediastinal pleura behind the sternum, and the costodiaphragmatic recess, between costal and diaphragmatic pleura at the outlet of the thorax.

## explicit_objective
Name the four regions of the parietal pleura and the two pleural recesses, and state the function of the pulmonary ligament.

## pitfalls
Treating the pulmonary ligament as attaching anything. It is an empty double fold of pleura below the lung root whose only job is to leave slack for the pulmonary veins to distend into when venous return rises.

# Item
## label
The cervical pleura rises 3 cm above the medial third of the clavicle, and the two anterior pleural borders diverge below the fourth costal cartilage for the cardiac notch

## id
CON-RES-B833146BBFCB26

## canonical_key
pleura.surface-anatomy

## definition
The cervical pleura is represented by a curved line, convex upwards, from the junction of the medial and middle thirds of the clavicle to the sternoclavicular joint, its highest point about 3 cm above the medial third of the clavicle. The anterior border descends from the sternoclavicular joint to the sternal angle identically on both sides; below that, the right border continues vertically to the xiphisternal junction while the left deviates laterally at the fourth costal cartilage to leave room for the heart before rejoining the sternal margin at the sixth cartilage. The inferior border runs obliquely backwards, crossing the eighth rib in the midclavicular line, the tenth in the midaxillary line and the twelfth spine posteriorly, and the posterior border ascends vertically alongside the vertebral column from the twelfth to the first thoracic spine.

## explicit_objective
Reproduce the surface markings of the four borders of the pleura, including where the right and left anterior borders diverge.

## pitfalls
Giving the pleura's inferior border the same line as the lung's. The pleura extends about two ribs lower than the lung at each of the same three reference lines, which is exactly the margin the costodiaphragmatic recess occupies.

# Item
## label
Pneumothorax, haemothorax, pyothorax and pleural effusion are the four abnormal fillings of the pleural cavity, drained by needle in the seventh intercostal space in the midaxillary line

## id
CON-RES-7EDFFAB60101ED

## canonical_key
pleural-cavity.abnormal-fillings-and-aspiration

## definition
The pleural cavity can fill abnormally with air (pneumothorax, from a lung perforation or chest wound, which collapses the lung), blood (haemothorax), pus (pyothorax or empyema) or excess serous fluid (pleural effusion). Fluid is drained by a wide-bore needle placed in the seventh intercostal space in the midaxillary line — not lower, to avoid the diaphragm — and midway between the upper and lower boundaries of the space, to avoid the neurovascular bundle at the lower border of the rib above and the collateral vessels at the upper border of the rib below.

## explicit_objective
Name the four abnormal fillings of the pleural cavity and state the safe site and plane for aspirating pleural fluid.

## pitfalls
Placing the aspiration needle against either boundary of the intercostal space. The safe plane is the middle of the space — too high risks the vein, artery and nerve under the rib above; too low risks the collateral vessels along the rib below.

# Item
## label
Each lung is half a cone with an apex, a base, costal and medial surfaces, and anterior, posterior and inferior borders

## id
CON-RES-69F499B794713C

## canonical_key
lung.external-features

## definition
Each lung resembles half a cone, with an apex projecting through the thoracic inlet to about one inch above the medial third of the clavicle, and a concave base resting on the diaphragm. It has two surfaces — a wide convex costal surface against the ribs, and a medial surface bearing the hilum, divided into a vertebral part related to the vertebral bodies, intercostal nerves and sympathetic chain, and a mediastinal part carrying the hilum and the grooves for neighbouring structures — and three borders: a thin, sharp anterior border, notched by the first rib above and, on the left, carrying the cardiac notch and lingula below; a thick, rounded posterior border; and an inferior border encircling the base. Before birth the lung is firm and sinks in water; after the first breath it becomes spongy and floats, which gives the finding medico-legal importance.

## explicit_objective
Name the parts of a lung — apex, base, two surfaces, three borders — and state the fetal-versus-postnatal buoyancy test and its medico-legal use.

## pitfalls
Assuming both lungs have an identical anterior border. Only the right border is straight; the left carries the cardiac notch and lingula, which is the single biggest external difference between the two lungs' borders.

# Item
## label
The root of the lung is enclosed in a pleural sleeve and its structures are arranged with the superior pulmonary vein most anterior, the principal bronchus most posterior and the artery in front of the bronchus

## id
CON-RES-9E5D1C2A67CB5D

## canonical_key
lung.hilum-and-root

## definition
The hilum is the area on the mediastinal surface through which the structures of the root enter or leave the lung; the root is that group of structures, sleeved by a downward prolongation of mediastinal pleura that continues below as the pulmonary ligament. In both lungs the superior pulmonary vein lies most anteriorly, the inferior pulmonary vein most inferiorly, and the principal bronchus most posteriorly. The pulmonary artery lies in front of the bronchus, above it on the left and between the bronchus and superior pulmonary vein on the right. Bronchial vessels, the anterior and posterior pulmonary plexuses, and bronchopulmonary lymph nodes and vessels fill the spaces between these named structures. The right principal bronchus divides before it reaches the hilum into an eparterial bronchus to the upper lobe and a hyparterial bronchus to the middle and lower lobes.

## explicit_objective
Give the vertical and antero-posterior order of the structures at the root of the lung, and state where the pulmonary artery sits relative to the bronchus on each side.

## pitfalls
Placing the pulmonary artery in the same position on both sides. On the right it lies between the bronchus and the superior pulmonary vein; on the left it lies above and in front of the bronchus — a genuine left–right asymmetry, not a slip.

# Item
## label
The oblique fissure is present in both lungs; only the right lung also has a horizontal fissure, which separates its upper and middle lobes

## id
CON-RES-DC7CC9CC4757BE

## canonical_key
lung.fissures-and-lobes

## definition
Both lungs have an oblique fissure, running from the posterior border 6 cm below the apex down and forwards to the inferior border, cutting as far as the hilum. The right lung alone also has a horizontal fissure, running from the anterior border to meet the oblique fissure at the sixth rib in the midaxillary line, and separating the upper from the middle lobe. The right lung therefore has three lobes — upper, middle and lower, the lower being larger than the other two combined — while the left has only an upper lobe, equivalent to the right's upper and middle together and carrying the cardiac notch and lingula, and a lower lobe identical in extent to the right's.

## explicit_objective
State which fissure is unique to the right lung and name the lobes each fissure produces on each side.

## pitfalls
Assuming the left lung has no equivalent of the right middle lobe at all. It has no separate lobe, but the lingula of its upper lobe occupies the same territory the middle lobe occupies on the right — the anatomical position is analogous even though the lobar division is not.

# Item
## label
A bronchopulmonary segment is a wedge-shaped, functionally independent unit supplied by its own segmental bronchus and pulmonary artery branch, ten on the right and eight on the left

## id
CON-RES-2A560C53F8E712

## canonical_key
bronchopulmonary-segment.definition-and-distribution

## definition
A bronchopulmonary segment is a functionally independent unit of lung tissue supplied by a segmental (tertiary) bronchus and an accompanying end-artery branch of the pulmonary artery, with its own lymphatics and autonomic innervation. Each segment is wedge-shaped, apex at the hilum and base at the lung surface, separated from its neighbours by connective-tissue septa that carry the intersegmental radicles of the pulmonary veins. The trachea divides into two main bronchi, each dividing into lobar (secondary) bronchi — three on the right, two on the left — which divide again into segmental bronchi: ten on the right and eight on the left, the left having fewer because its apical and posterior segments share a common stem and its medial basal segment is frequently absent, though the numbering is kept identical on both sides for comparison.

## explicit_objective
Define a bronchopulmonary segment by its bronchus, artery and vein relationships, and give the segment count on each side with the reason the left has fewer.

## pitfalls
Assuming the pulmonary vein tributaries run inside the segment like the artery and bronchus. They are intersegmental, running in the connective-tissue septa between segments, which is exactly why a segmentectomy can remove one segment cleanly along the venous planes.

# Item
## label
The lung has a dual blood supply: pulmonary vessels carry blood to be oxygenated, and bronchial vessels nourish the lung tissue itself

## id
CON-RES-CF9852282948CD

## canonical_key
lung.dual-blood-supply

## definition
The lung receives two separate circulations. The functional, pulmonary circulation carries deoxygenated blood from the right ventricle by one pulmonary artery per lung, which branches with the bronchial tree as far as the alveolar capillaries as end arteries with no anastomoses between segments, and returns oxygenated blood by two pulmonary veins, superior and inferior, per lung. The nutritive, bronchial circulation supplies the bronchial tree, stroma and visceral pleura: the right lung by one bronchial artery from the third right posterior intercostal artery (or the upper left bronchial artery), the left lung by two bronchial arteries from the descending thoracic aorta, with bronchial veins draining the right lung to the azygos vein and the left to the superior hemiazygos vein.

## explicit_objective
Distinguish the pulmonary from the bronchial circulation of the lung by origin, direction of flow and what each supplies.

## pitfalls
Treating 'blood supply of the lung' as one system. A question naming only the pulmonary artery and veins has answered function, not nutrition — the bronchial arteries are the ones an examiner is testing when the question turns to the lung's own tissue.

# Item
## label
Lymph from the lung drains centripetally from the pleura to the hilum through bronchopulmonary then tracheobronchial nodes, reaching the thoracic duct on the left and the right brachiocephalic vein on the right

## id
CON-RES-08EA4617DB3751

## canonical_key
lung.lymphatic-drainage

## definition
The alveolar walls have no lymphatics; interstitial lymph vessels drain centripetally, from the visceral pleura at the periphery towards the hilum at the centre, starting in the walls of the bronchioles and running alongside the segmental and lobar bronchi. Lymph from the lung parenchyma passes first through intrapulmonary lymph follicles to the bronchopulmonary nodes at the hilum, then to the tracheobronchial nodes at the tracheal bifurcation, then to the paratracheal nodes, which are drained by the mediastinal lymph trunk — opening into the right brachiocephalic vein on the right side and into the thoracic duct on the left.

## explicit_objective
Trace lymph from the alveolar wall to its final venous entry point, naming each node group in order and the side-dependent final drainage.

## pitfalls
Looking for lymphatics in the alveolar wall itself. The book states plainly that the walls of the alveoli are devoid of lymph vessels — drainage begins one level up, in the walls of the bronchioles.

# Item
## label
The right lung is shorter, broader and heavier than the left, has three lobes and two fissures against the left's two lobes and one, and ten bronchopulmonary segments against eight

## id
CON-RES-491F7739C10120

## canonical_key
right-vs-left-lung.differences

## definition
The right lung is shorter than the left because the liver raises the right cupola of the diaphragm, but broader because the heart bulges to the left, and it is heavier with a greater capacity. Its anterior border is straight, unlike the left's cardiac notch and lingula, and its base is less concave. It has two fissures and three lobes against the left's one fissure and two lobes. At the hilum its superior lobar bronchus is eparterial, arising outside the lung and above the pulmonary artery, while the left's arises inside the lung; its pulmonary artery lies between the bronchus and the superior pulmonary vein rather than above and in front of the bronchus as on the left; and it has one bronchial artery against the left's two. It has ten bronchopulmonary segments against the left's eight, the difference explained by a shared apicoposterior stem and a frequently absent medial basal segment on the left.

## explicit_objective
List the differences between the right and left lungs across shape, borders, fissures and lobes, hilar arrangement, and segment count.

## pitfalls
Explaining the shorter right lung by the heart rather than the liver. The book attributes the shorter right lung to the higher right cupola of the diaphragm, raised by the liver; the heart is why the right lung is broader, a separate fact about a separate dimension.

# Item
## label
The mediastinal surfaces of the two lungs carry different impressions: the right is grooved by the SVC and azygos arch, the left by the aortic arch and its two great branches

## id
CON-RES-DC1111DA6DD151

## canonical_key
lung.mediastinal-surface-impressions

## definition
On the right lung's mediastinal surface, in front of and above the hilum lie the cardiac impression (related to the right atrium and auricle), the superior vena caval groove, the impression for the ascending aorta, the inferior vena caval groove, the groove for the arch of the azygos vein and the tracheal and oesophageal grooves, the latter continuing behind the hilum. On the left lung's mediastinal surface, the cardiac impression is related mainly to the left ventricle and atrium; above the hilum lie the broad groove for the arch of the aorta — separated from the lung by the left phrenic and vagus nerves, the superficial cardiac plexus and the left superior intercostal vein — and the grooves for the left common carotid and left subclavian arteries, with the oesophageal groove behind the subclavian groove; behind the hilum lies the groove for the descending thoracic aorta.

## explicit_objective
Name the vascular impressions above and in front of the hilum on the right lung and the left, and state the five structures separating the aortic arch groove from the left lung.

## pitfalls
Placing the ascending aorta's impression on the left lung. It is a right-lung impression, triangular and anterior to the hilum; the left lung's equivalent anterior impression is the pulmonary trunk, a different vessel entirely.

# Item
## label
The diaphragm arises from sternal, costal and vertebral origins — the vertebral part by two crura and five arcuate ligaments — and converges on a trifoliate central tendon

## id
CON-RES-6023E5A5A7161F

## canonical_key
diaphragm.origin-and-central-tendon

## definition
The diaphragm is a dome-shaped fibromuscular partition, convex upwards, with a higher right cupola raised by the liver. It arises from three areas: a sternal origin by two fleshy slips from the xiphoid process, a costal origin from the deep surface of the lower six costal cartilages interdigitating with transversus abdominis, and a vertebral origin by two fleshy crura — the right, larger, from the upper three lumbar vertebrae and surrounding the oesophagus as a physiological sphincter, the left from the upper two — and five arcuate ligaments: one median, arching over the aorta between the crura, and two each of medial (over psoas major) and lateral (over quadratus lumborum). All the fibres converge, with no bony insertion, on the central tendon, a trifoliate aponeurosis whose median leaf is related to the pericardium and heart.

## explicit_objective
Name the three origins of the diaphragm, distinguish the right crus from the left, and state what the central tendon is and is not attached to.

## pitfalls
Giving the diaphragm a bony insertion. It has none — every fibre, from all three origins, converges on the central tendon, which is itself an aponeurosis, not bone.

# Item
## label
The diaphragm has three major openings — aortic at T12, oesophageal at T10, caval at T8 — plus named minor openings for the musculophrenic artery, splanchnic nerves and phrenic nerves

## id
CON-RES-80C9B9E43B459B

## canonical_key
diaphragm.major-openings

## definition
The three major openings of the diaphragm are stacked at three vertebral levels in the midline plane, each transmitting a distinct set of structures: the aortic opening, behind the median arcuate ligament at T12, transmitting the aorta, the azygos vein to its right and the thoracic duct between them; the oesophageal opening, in the right crus at T10 one inch left of the midline, transmitting the oesophagus, the two vagi and the oesophageal branches of the left gastric vessels; and the caval opening, in the central tendon at T8 one inch right of the midline, transmitting the inferior vena cava, the right phrenic nerve and lymphatics. Minor openings transmit the musculophrenic artery between the seventh and eighth costal slips, the superior epigastric artery between the sternal and costal origins, the lower five intercostal nerves and vessels between the costal digitations, the subcostal nerve and vessels and quadratus lumborum behind the lateral arcuate ligament, the hemiazygos vein through the left crus, the sympathetic chain and psoas major behind the medial arcuate ligament, the splanchnic nerves through the crura, and the left phrenic nerve through the left cupola.

## explicit_objective
State the vertebral level and the structures transmitted by each of the three major diaphragmatic openings, in order from T8 to T12.

## pitfalls
Mixing up which structure sits to the left and which to the right at the aortic opening. The azygos vein lies to the right of the aorta and the thoracic duct lies between them — reversing left and right here is the commonest slip.

# Item
## label
The phrenic nerve is the sole motor supply of the diaphragm, arising in the neck from C3, 4 and 5 and supplying the abdominal surface because the embryo folds

## id
CON-RES-449B5BD0266989

## canonical_key
diaphragm.nerve-supply-and-action

## definition
The right and left phrenic nerves, arising in the cervical region from C3, 4 and 5, are the only motor supply of the diaphragm; they descend to ramify on its inferior (abdominal) surface, which they supply because folding of the embryo turns what was the diaphragm's cranial surface to face caudally. Sensory supply is mainly to the peritoneum and pleura related to the diaphragm — the phrenic nerves to its central part, the lower six thoracic spinal nerves to the peripheral parts — with only a few proprioceptive fibres from the muscle itself. As the chief muscle of respiration the diaphragm also raises intra-abdominal pressure for parturition, coughing, defecation and micturition.

## explicit_objective
State the root value and the sole motor role of the phrenic nerve, and explain why it supplies the diaphragm's inferior rather than superior surface.

## pitfalls
Giving the phrenic nerve only a motor role. It also carries sensory fibres — to the central diaphragmatic pleura and peritoneum — while the lower six thoracic nerves take the peripheral sensory territory instead.

# Item
## label
Diaphragmatic hernia may occur through the foramen of Morgagni between sternal and costal origins, through the foramen of Bochdalek between costal and vertebral origins, or through a widened oesophageal opening as a hiatus hernia

## id
CON-RES-A9AE09CC34A162

## canonical_key
diaphragmatic-hernia.types-and-sites

## definition
Small gaps between the diaphragm's areas of origin, normally filled with loose areolar tissue, are the sites of its three named hernias. The foramen of Morgagni lies between the sternal and costal origins and transmits the superior epigastric vessels; it may be the site of an anterior, parasternal hernia. The foramen of Bochdalek, or vertebrocostal triangle, lies between the costal and vertebral origins, mainly on the left, where the pleura of the costodiaphragmatic recess is separated from the kidney only by areolar tissue; it is the remnant of the fetal pleuroperitoneal canal and the site of a posterior hernia. A hiatus hernia is the passage of the stomach through the oesophageal opening.

## explicit_objective
Name the three types of diaphragmatic hernia and the anatomical gap or opening each passes through.

## pitfalls
Placing the foramen of Bochdalek symmetrically on both sides. The book states it lies mainly on the left, which is the reason a left-sided congenital diaphragmatic hernia is the classic teaching case.

# Item
## label
A plane from the sternal angle to the T4/T5 disc divides the mediastinum into superior and inferior parts, and the inferior part into anterior, middle and posterior

## id
CON-RES-F78FBA0BF1E673

## canonical_key
mediastinum.subdivisions-and-boundaries

## definition
The mediastinum is the thick median partition of the thoracic cavity, extending from the sternum to the vertebral column and from the thoracic inlet to the diaphragm. An imaginary plane from the sternal angle in front to the lower border of T4, or the T4/T5 disc, behind divides it into a superior mediastinum above and an inferior mediastinum below. The inferior mediastinum is further subdivided into an anterior mediastinum, a narrow space in front of the pericardium; a middle mediastinum, occupied by the pericardium and its contents; and a posterior mediastinum, behind the pericardium and in front of the vertebral column. Between them the four compartments hold the pericardium and heart, the great arteries and veins of the thorax, the vagus and phrenic nerves, the lymph nodes and thoracic duct, and the trachea and oesophagus.

## explicit_objective
Draw the plane that divides superior from inferior mediastinum and name the three subdivisions of the inferior mediastinum.

## pitfalls
Placing the dividing plane at the sternal angle alone. It runs from the sternal angle in front to the T4/T5 disc behind — an oblique plane, not a horizontal line at one level.

# Item
## label
The superior mediastinum holds the great veins and thymus in front, the aortic arch and its three branches in the middle, and the trachea, oesophagus and thoracic duct behind

## id
CON-RES-F360EFDEA84D58

## canonical_key
superior-mediastinum.contents

## definition
The superior mediastinum lies between the manubrium in front, the upper four thoracic vertebrae behind, the thoracic inlet above and the sternal angle plane below. Retrosternally it holds the two brachiocephalic veins, the upper half of the superior vena cava, the left superior intercostal vein and the thymus gland; more centrally, the arch of the aorta and its three branches — brachiocephalic, left common carotid and left subclavian — occupying the concavity of which sits the bifurcation of the pulmonary trunk; the two vagi and phrenic nerves and the left recurrent laryngeal nerve; and, most posteriorly, the trachea, the oesophagus behind it, and the thoracic duct ascending behind the oesophagus's left border.

## explicit_objective
List the four groups of structures in the superior mediastinum from front to back and name one member of each group.

## pitfalls
Placing the thoracic duct in front of the oesophagus. The book's front-to-back order for the superior mediastinum ends with trachea, then oesophagus, then thoracic duct — the duct is the most posterior of the three tubal structures, not the trachea.

# Item
## label
The middle mediastinum is the pericardium and everything it encloses: the heart, the ascending aorta, the pulmonary trunk, the lower SVC, the upper IVC and the pulmonary veins

## id
CON-CVS-83DB0089BA4BD1

## canonical_key
middle-mediastinum.contents

## definition
The middle mediastinum lies between the anterior mediastinum in front and the posterior mediastinum behind, from the superior mediastinum above to the diaphragm below, flanked by pleura and lung on each side. Its contents are the pericardium and the structures it encloses: the heart itself, the ascending aorta, the pulmonary trunk lying to the ascending aorta's left, the lower half of the superior vena cava lying to its right, the uppermost part of the inferior vena cava, and the terminal parts of the two right and two left pulmonary veins.

## explicit_objective
State the boundaries of the middle mediastinum and list the six named structures its contents divide into (pericardium plus five enclosed structures).

## pitfalls
Listing the whole heart and great vessels as free contents of the mediastinum without naming the pericardium as the container. The book's list is structured as pericardium first, then what it encloses — the pericardium is the defining content, not an afterthought.

# Item
## label
The transverse sinus of the pericardium passes between the arterial and venous ends of the heart; the oblique sinus is a blind recess behind the left atrium

## id
CON-CVS-3706498A4F8F97

## canonical_key
pericardium.transverse-and-oblique-sinuses

## definition
The visceral layer of serous pericardium sends two tube-like extensions over the roots of the great vessels: an anterior tube enclosing the ascending aorta and pulmonary trunk together, and a posterior tube enclosing parts of the superior and inferior venae cavae and the four pulmonary veins. Between these two tubes lies the transverse sinus, a passage connecting the right and left sides of the pericardial cavity between the arterial end of the heart in front and the venous end behind — bounded above and behind by the right pulmonary artery and below and behind by the superior vena cava and the two atria, mainly the left; a finger passed behind the ascending aorta and pulmonary trunk and in front of the lower superior vena cava traverses it. The oblique sinus is a blind recess extending upwards between the back of the left atrium in front and the fibrous pericardium behind, closed above by the pericardial reflection onto the left atrium and open below into the main pericardial cavity between the inferior vena cava and the lower left pulmonary vein.

## explicit_objective
Describe how to reach the transverse sinus and the oblique sinus with a finger, and name what closes the oblique sinus above.

## pitfalls
Confusing which sinus is a through passage and which is a dead end. The transverse sinus connects right and left sides of the pericardial cavity; the oblique sinus is blind at its upper end and opens only into the main cavity below — a finger placed in one cannot pass into the other, which is exactly how the book distinguishes them on a cadaver.

# Item
## label
The fibrous and parietal serous pericardium are somatically sensitive to pain via the phrenic nerve, while the visceral layer shares the heart's autonomic supply and its coronary blood supply

## id
CON-CVS-2DB020EDD28EB7

## canonical_key
pericardium.nerve-and-blood-supply

## definition
The fibrous pericardium and the parietal layer of serous pericardium are supplied by the phrenic nerves, which carry somatic sensation and make this pericardium sensitive to pain, as in pericarditis; their arterial supply is from the internal thoracic arteries and their pericardiacophrenic and musculophrenic branches and from the descending aorta, with venous drainage through the azygos system. The visceral layer of serous pericardium, the epicardium, is supplied like the rest of the heart by autonomic sympathetic and parasympathetic fibres and is insensitive to pain but sensitive to ischaemia; its blood supply is from the right and left coronary arteries, draining by the coronary sinus.

## explicit_objective
Contrast the nerve supply of the fibrous and parietal pericardium with that of the visceral layer, and state which layer is pain-sensitive.

## pitfalls
Assuming the whole pericardium is pain-sensitive because pericarditis hurts. Only the fibrous and parietal layers, supplied by the phrenic nerve, carry pain sensation; the visceral layer (epicardium) shares the heart's own autonomic supply and feels ischaemia, not pain.

# Item
## label
Fluid drained from the pericardial cavity is aspirated in the fifth or sixth left intercostal space near the sternum, avoiding the pleura and internal thoracic artery

## id
CON-CVS-32B764922AF233

## canonical_key
pericardiocentesis.site-and-clinical-basis

## definition
Fluid collecting in the pericardial cavity — a pericardial effusion — hinders the heart's movements and causes severe distress; it is drained by needle paracentesis. The puncture is made in the fifth or sixth intercostal space close to the sternum, a site chosen to avoid injuring the pleura or the internal thoracic artery, both of which run nearby.

## explicit_objective
Give the intercostal space and the site of pericardiocentesis, and name the two structures the site is chosen to avoid.

## pitfalls
Choosing a puncture site far from the sternum for a wider working space. The book's site is deliberately close to the sternum, precisely because that is where the internal thoracic artery and the pleural reflection are furthest away.

# Item
## label
The heart lies obliquely in the middle mediastinum, one third to the right and two thirds to the left of the median plane, with a base, an apex, two surfaces and four borders

## id
CON-CVS-74C0F9BB0D3490

## canonical_key
heart.external-features

## definition
The heart is a hollow muscular pump lying inside the pericardium in the middle mediastinum, behind the sternum and adjoining costal cartilages, one third of its bulk to the right of the median plane and two thirds to the left. It is roughly the size of its owner's closed fist, about 12 cm from base to apex, 8 cm side to side and 6 cm front to back, and weighs about 300 g in males and 250 g in females. It is conical, with a base (posterior surface, formed mainly by the left atrium), an apex (formed wholly by the left ventricle, the lowest and leftmost point of the heart, opposite the left fifth intercostal space), a sternocostal (anterior) and a diaphragmatic (inferior) surface, and four borders — superior, inferior, right and left — separated on the surface by the coronary sulcus into atrial and ventricular parts, and on front and back by the anterior and inferior interventricular grooves between the two ventricles.

## explicit_objective
State the heart's position relative to the median plane, and name its base, apex, two surfaces and four borders and which chamber forms each.

## pitfalls
Forgetting that the apex is entirely left ventricle. A question naming 'the apex' is naming the left ventricle by another name, which is why apex beat findings are read as left ventricular findings.

# Item
## label
The right atrium is split by the crista terminalis into a rough atrium proper and a smooth sinus venarum; the right ventricle has an inflow part with three papillary muscles and a smooth infundibulum

## id
CON-CVS-42C907CE4749DC

## canonical_key
heart.right-chambers-interior

## definition
The right atrium's cavity is divided by the crista terminalis, a muscular ridge between the openings of the superior and inferior venae cavae, into an anterior rough atrium proper, roughened by the comb-like musculi pectinati, and a posterior smooth sinus venarum, which receives the superior vena cava (no valve), the inferior vena cava (a rudimentary valve) and the coronary sinus (a small valve). Its septal wall carries the fossa ovalis and its raised margin, the annulus ovalis, deficient below. The right ventricle's inflow part, entered through the tricuspid orifice, has coarse trabeculae carneae and three papillary muscles — anterior, posterior and septal — whose chordae tendinae anchor the three tricuspid cusps, and a septomarginal trabecula (moderator band) running from the septum to the anterior papillary muscle, both bracing the ventricle and carrying the right bundle branch. Its smooth outflow part, the infundibulum, leads to the pulmonary orifice, guarded by a three-cusped pulmonary valve with two anterior cusps and one posterior.

## explicit_objective
Name the structure that separates the rough from the smooth part of the right atrium, and describe the three papillary muscles and the moderator band of the right ventricle.

## pitfalls
Assuming the moderator band is purely structural. It carries the right bundle branch of the conducting system as well as bracing the ventricle against overdistension — a two-function structure, not one.

# Item
## label
The left atrium receives four pulmonary veins and is mostly smooth; the left ventricle's inflow part has two papillary muscles feeding the mitral valve and its outflow part is the fibrous aortic vestibule

## id
CON-CVS-ECDC97B24BB5DE

## canonical_key
heart.left-chambers-interior

## definition
The left atrium, smaller than the right, forms most of the base and upper border of the heart; it is smooth except for a few musculi pectinati in its auricle, and receives the four pulmonary veins on its posterior wall in addition to the left atrioventricular orifice. The left ventricle, with a wall three times thicker than the right ventricle's, has a rough inflow part with fine trabeculae carneae and two papillary muscles — anterior and posterior — whose chordae attach to both cusps of the mitral valve, and a smooth outflow part, the aortic vestibule, formed of fibrous tissue to stay patent through systole and leading to the aortic orifice, guarded by an aortic valve of two posterior and one anterior cusp. Blood flow through the right ventricle turns nearly a right angle between inflow and outflow; through the left ventricle the turn is an acute angle.

## explicit_objective
Name the two papillary muscles of the left ventricle and the valve their chordae serve, and contrast the angle blood turns through in the right versus the left ventricle.

## pitfalls
Giving the left ventricle three papillary muscles by analogy with the right. It has only two, anterior and posterior, matching its valve's two cusps — the right ventricle's third, septal, papillary muscle has no left-sided counterpart.

# Item
## label
The fibrous skeleton of the heart is the dense collagenous framework at the base of the ventricles that carries the four valve rings and electrically isolates atria from ventricles

## id
CON-CVS-F997D0CCBA3161

## canonical_key
heart.fibrous-skeleton

## definition
The fibrous skeleton sits in the plane of the atrioventricular sulcus at the base of the two ventricles, built of dense collagenous bundles. Its parts are the right and left atrioventricular rings, together forming an 8-shaped structure with the right larger but weaker; the fibrous rings of the pulmonary and aortic orifices, giving attachment to the valve cusps; the right and left fibrous trigones, masses of tissue between the aortic ring and each atrioventricular ring, the right the larger; and the tendon of the infundibulum, connecting the infundibulum to the aortic ring. It gives stable attachment to the valve cusps and to the atrial and ventricular musculature, and ensures electrophysiological discontinuity between atrial and ventricular myocardium — the atrioventricular bundle is the one structure that is allowed to cross it.

## explicit_objective
Name the four components of the fibrous skeleton and state the one structure that is permitted to cross it electrically.

## pitfalls
Treating the fibrous skeleton as purely a mechanical support. Its third function — keeping atrial and ventricular myocardium electrically discontinuous — is why the AV bundle has to exist at all as the sole conducting bridge.

# Item
## label
The conducting system runs SA node to AV node to AV bundle to right and left bundle branches to Purkinje fibres, arranged so each ventricle contracts from the apex upwards

## id
CON-CVS-6799821893D6D2

## canonical_key
conducting-system-of-heart.components

## definition
The conducting system is built of specialised cardiac muscle, not nerve tissue. The sino-atrial node, in the right atrial wall behind the SVC opening, is the pacemaker that initiates each cardiac impulse. The atrio-ventricular node, in the lower posterior interatrial septum above the coronary sinus opening, relays it to the atrioventricular bundle (bundle of His), the sole muscular bridge across the fibrous skeleton, which descends along the membranous septum before splitting into a right branch — running under the septal endocardium to the moderator band and the anterior papillary muscle — and a left branch, running under the septal endocardium towards the apex. Both branches break into a Purkinje network that encircles the papillary muscle bases and ascends beneath the endocardium towards the ventricular base, so each ventricle contracts from below upwards and empties into its outflow artery rather than trapping blood.

## explicit_objective
Name the five components of the conducting system in order from the SA node to the Purkinje fibres, and explain why the ventricles contract from apex to base.

## pitfalls
Treating the conducting system as nervous tissue. The book states explicitly it is formed of specialised cardiac muscle fibres, not nerve tissue — the autonomic nerves only modulate its rate, they do not form it.

# Item
## label
The right coronary artery runs the coronary sulcus to give the marginal and posterior interventricular arteries, supplying the whole right side of the heart and, in most people, the whole conducting system

## id
CON-CVS-2A21F1B4F30B61

## canonical_key
right-coronary-artery.course-and-distribution

## definition
The right coronary artery arises from the anterior aortic sinus, passes between the pulmonary trunk and right auricle to the anterior coronary sulcus, descends to the junction of the right and inferior borders where it gives the marginal artery, then turns back along the posterior sulcus to give the posterior (inferior) interventricular artery — which also supplies the AV node and bundle — before anastomosing with the circumflex artery. It supplies the whole right atrium, the whole right ventricle except a strip beside the anterior interventricular groove, a strip of the left ventricle's diaphragmatic surface beside the posterior interventricular groove, the posterior third of the interventricular septum, and the whole conducting system in most people: the SA node in 60% and the AV node and bundle branches in 80%. When its posterior interventricular branch is the one present, the heart is called right-dominant, the commonest pattern.

## explicit_objective
Trace the right coronary artery's course from its aortic sinus origin to its anastomosis, and state which parts of the conducting system it supplies and in what proportion of people.

## pitfalls
Assuming the right coronary artery always supplies the SA node. It does so in only 60% of people; the other 40% take their SA nodal artery from the left coronary artery, which is why coronary dominance is described as a pattern, not a rule.

# Item
## label
The left coronary artery divides into the anterior interventricular and circumflex arteries, together supplying the left atrium, most of the left ventricle and the anterior two thirds of the septum

## id
CON-CVS-1F1AB4B70AB06D

## canonical_key
left-coronary-artery.branches-and-distribution

## definition
The left coronary artery, larger than the right, arises from the left posterior aortic sinus, passes between the pulmonary trunk and left auricle to the top of the anterior interventricular groove, and ends by dividing into the anterior interventricular artery — which descends to the inferior border, turns round it and anastomoses with the right coronary artery's posterior interventricular branch, supplying the sternocostal surface of the left ventricle, a strip of the right ventricle and the anterior two thirds of the septum — and the circumflex artery, which supplies the left atrium and, through its marginal branch, the left ventricle down to the apex, and the SA node in 40% and the AV node in 20% of people. Together the two branches supply the whole left atrium, the whole left ventricle except a strip on its diaphragmatic surface, a strip of the right ventricle's sternocostal surface, and the anterior two thirds of the interventricular septum.

## explicit_objective
Name the two terminal branches of the left coronary artery and state what each supplies, including its share of the conducting system.

## pitfalls
Crediting the anterior interventricular artery with the posterior third of the septum. That third belongs to the right coronary artery; the anterior interventricular artery supplies the anterior two thirds only.

# Item
## label
Most cardiac veins end in the coronary sinus, which lies in the posterior coronary sulcus and receives the great, middle and small cardiac veins and the oblique vein of the left atrium

## id
CON-CVS-929D9DDCB95482

## canonical_key
coronary-venous-drainage.coronary-sinus-and-cardiac-veins

## definition
The coronary sinus is a short, wide venous channel in the posterior coronary sulcus, beginning as the continuation of the great cardiac vein and opening into the right atrium between the IVC opening and the tricuspid orifice, guarded by a small valve. Its tributaries are the great cardiac vein, which ascends in the anterior interventricular groove with the artery of the same name and curves back in the anterior sulcus to reach the sinus's left end; the middle cardiac vein, running back in the posterior interventricular groove to join the sinus at its middle; the small cardiac vein, running up the sternocostal surface near the inferior border with the marginal artery to join the sinus's right end; and the oblique vein of the left atrium, descending on the back of the left atrium to join near the sinus's left end. A separate set of veins bypasses the sinus entirely: three or four anterior cardiac veins on the right ventricle's sternocostal surface, and minute venae cordis minimae in the myocardium, both opening directly into the right atrium.

## explicit_objective
Name the four tributaries of the coronary sinus and the two types of cardiac vein that open directly into a chamber instead.

## pitfalls
Assuming every cardiac vein drains through the coronary sinus. The anterior cardiac veins and the venae cordis minimae bypass it entirely and open straight into the right atrium — a real exception, not an approximation.

# Item
## label
The heart's autonomic supply is parasympathetic from the two vagi and sympathetic from the upper five thoracic segments, meeting in superficial and deep cardiac plexuses that only modulate rate, never initiate it

## id
CON-CVS-B12EDEA332E53F

## canonical_key
heart.nerve-supply-and-cardiac-plexuses

## definition
The heart's rate and contractile strength are controlled by autonomic fibres, sympathetic increasing both and parasympathetic decreasing them; neither initiates or propagates the cardiac impulse, which is the conducting system's job, though the plexuses also carry afferent sympathetic pain fibres from the heart. Parasympathetic preganglionic fibres arise from the vagal nuclei and travel in cervical and thoracic cardiac branches of the vagi and the left recurrent laryngeal nerve, relaying in ganglia in the heart wall. Sympathetic preganglionic fibres arise in the lateral horn of the upper five thoracic segments, relay in the cervical and upper five thoracic sympathetic ganglia, and travel onward as postganglionic cardiac branches. These converge in the superficial cardiac plexus, in the concavity of the aortic arch on the ligamentum arteriosum, and the deep cardiac plexus, in front of the tracheal bifurcation deep to the arch, from which branches pass to the atria and to right and left coronary and pulmonary plexuses.

## explicit_objective
State which autonomic division increases and which decreases heart rate, and name the two cardiac plexuses and where each lies.

## pitfalls
Crediting the autonomic nerves with starting the heartbeat. The book is explicit that they are not responsible for initiation or propagation of the impulse — only the SA node and conducting system do that; the nerves only speed it up or slow it down.

# Item
## label
The heart is fixed by the pericardium's own attachments and by a sling mechanism in which the great vessels hook around the bronchi, leaving the ventricles free to contract

## id
CON-CVS-82C57174816F98

## canonical_key
heart.fixation-mechanisms

## definition
The heart is held in position by four mechanisms: fixation of the pericardium to surrounding structures, hooking of its great vessels around thoracic structures, lateral support from the pleurae and lungs, and support from below by the diaphragm. The pericardium is fixed anteriorly to the sternum by the sternopericardial ligaments, superiorly to the pretracheal fascia and carotid sheath (and so indirectly to the skull base), inferiorly by blending with the central tendon of the diaphragm, and laterally by blending with the mediastinal pleura. The cardiac sling adds hooking: the aortic arch and left pulmonary artery hook around the left principal bronchus, the arch of the azygos vein and right pulmonary artery hook around the right principal bronchus, and the subclavian vessels hang across the first rib on each side at the thoracic inlet — together fixing the base of the heart while leaving its ventricular part free to contract.

## explicit_objective
Name the four mechanisms that fix the heart in position and describe the three hooking relationships of the cardiac sling.

## pitfalls
Assuming the whole heart is rigidly fixed. The sling mechanism deliberately fixes only the base — the ventricular mass is left free, which is exactly what lets it contract without being restrained by its own suspension.

# Item
## label
The pulmonary trunk runs entirely within the fibrous pericardium and ends at the sternal angle level by dividing into right and left pulmonary arteries, joined to the aortic arch by the ligamentum arteriosum

## id
CON-CVS-A723ADE6F4E726

## canonical_key
pulmonary-trunk.origin-course-and-ligamentum-arteriosum

## definition
The pulmonary trunk carries deoxygenated blood from the right ventricle to the lungs. It begins at the pulmonary orifice at the summit of the infundibulum, behind the sternal end of the third left costal cartilage, runs 5 cm upwards, backwards and to the left winding round the ascending aorta's left side — its whole length inside the fibrous pericardium — and ends at the level of the sternal angle, below the aortic arch and in front of the trachea, by dividing into right and left pulmonary arteries, the right longer and wider. The ligamentum arteriosum, the obliterated fetal ductus arteriosus, is a flattened fibrous band between the left pulmonary artery and the concavity of the aortic arch, with the superficial cardiac plexus on its right anterior aspect and the left recurrent laryngeal nerve on its left posterior aspect.

## explicit_objective
State where the pulmonary trunk begins and ends, and name the two structures related to the ligamentum arteriosum on its right and left aspects.

## pitfalls
Placing part of the pulmonary trunk outside the fibrous pericardium. The book states its whole length lies within it, unlike the arch of the aorta, only part of which does.

# Item
## label
The ascending aorta is only 5 cm long, lies wholly within the fibrous pericardium, and gives no branches beyond the two coronary arteries from its aortic sinuses

## id
CON-CVS-D02DE9B56C97B7

## canonical_key
ascending-aorta.course-and-pericardial-relations

## definition
The ascending aorta is a short, wide artery, 5 cm long, beginning at the aortic orifice of the left ventricle behind the left sternal border opposite the third left intercostal space, running obliquely upwards, forwards and to the right, and ending behind the second right sternocostal junction by becoming the arch of the aorta. Its whole length lies inside the fibrous pericardium, and at its beginning it presents three dilatations opposite the aortic valve cusps, the aortic sinuses — one anterior, two posterior — from which the right and left coronary arteries arise as its only branches.

## explicit_objective
State the length of the ascending aorta, where it lies relative to the fibrous pericardium, and where its beginning and end sit in surface terms.

## pitfalls
Looking for branches of the ascending aorta beyond the coronary arteries. The book gives it exactly two branches, both coronary, both arising from its aortic sinuses — there is nothing else to name.

# Item
## label
The arch of the aorta passes over the left bronchus and gives the brachiocephalic, left common carotid and left subclavian arteries from its convexity, with the trachea and oesophagus behind it

## id
CON-CVS-9C60987F3CB5A1

## canonical_key
arch-of-aorta.relations-and-branches

## definition
The arch of the aorta continues the ascending aorta from the second right sternocostal junction, passing upwards, backwards and to the left in front of the trachea, then backwards and downwards on its left side, to end on the left of the T4/T5 disc as the descending thoracic aorta. Its upper convex aspect gives its three branches — brachiocephalic, left common carotid and left subclavian, in that order — and is crossed by the left brachiocephalic vein; its lower concave aspect overlies the pulmonary trunk's bifurcation, the left principal bronchus, the ligamentum arteriosum, the superficial cardiac plexus and the left recurrent laryngeal nerve; its left anterior aspect is crossed by the left phrenic and vagus nerves, cardiac branches and the left superior intercostal vein, separating it from the left pleura and lung; and its right posterior aspect is related to the trachea, the deep cardiac plexus at the tracheal bifurcation, the oesophagus, the left recurrent laryngeal nerve and the thoracic duct.

## explicit_objective
Name the three branches of the aortic arch in order and the structures related to each of its four aspects.

## pitfalls
Placing the deep cardiac plexus on the arch's convexity with its branches. It sits on the arch's right posterior aspect, at the tracheal bifurcation, together with the trachea and oesophagus — a different aspect from the one carrying the three named branches.

# Item
## label
The descending thoracic aorta runs from T4/T5 to T12, giving nine pairs of posterior intercostal arteries, a subcostal pair, two left bronchial arteries and oesophageal branches

## id
CON-CVS-EEA29FB47CBB71

## canonical_key
descending-thoracic-aorta.course-relations-and-branches

## definition
The descending thoracic aorta, about 20 cm long, is the longest thoracic part of the aorta. It begins on the left of the T4/T5 disc as the continuation of the aortic arch and descends, first on the left of the T5 to T7 vertebral bodies, then in front of T8 to T12, ending at the lower border of T12 by passing through the diaphragm's aortic opening to become the abdominal aorta. Anteriorly it is related, from above down, to the left principal bronchus, the pericardium, the oesophagus and the diaphragm; posteriorly to the lower five vertebral bodies and the two hemiazygos veins, crossing at T8 and T9; on its right to the oesophagus above and the thoracic duct and azygos vein throughout; and on its left to the pleura and lung. Its branches are nine pairs of posterior intercostal arteries, from the third to the eleventh space, one pair of subcostal arteries, two left bronchial arteries, four or five oesophageal branches, and small twigs to the pericardium, mediastinum and diaphragm.

## explicit_objective
State the vertebral levels the descending thoracic aorta begins and ends at, and list its five groups of branches.

## pitfalls
Crediting the descending thoracic aorta with all eleven pairs of posterior intercostal arteries. It gives only the lower nine, from the third to the eleventh space — the first and second pairs come from the superior intercostal artery, a branch of the subclavian, not from the aorta itself.

# Item
## label
The two brachiocephalic veins drain the head, neck, upper limbs and thoracic wall, uniting behind the first right costal cartilage to form the superior vena cava

## id
CON-CVS-9CDFD3C60A2550

## canonical_key
brachiocephalic-veins.formation-and-course

## definition
The right and left brachiocephalic veins drain the upper limbs, head and neck, the anterior thoracic wall, the upper posterior thoracic wall, and lymph from the whole body, ending by uniting behind the lower border of the first right costal cartilage to form the superior vena cava. The right vein descends nearly vertically from the medial end of the right clavicle, receiving the right internal thoracic vein, the right first posterior intercostal vein and the right lymphatic duct. The left vein runs obliquely downwards and to the right behind the upper half of the manubrium, along the upper aspect of the aortic arch and in front of the origins of its three branches, receiving the left internal thoracic vein, the left first posterior intercostal vein, the left superior intercostal vein and the thoracic duct.

## explicit_objective
State where the two brachiocephalic veins unite and what forms there, and name the one tributary unique to each vein (the two lymphatic ducts).

## pitfalls
Giving the left brachiocephalic vein the same vertical course as the right. The left vein is markedly oblique, crossing in front of the aortic arch's three branches, which is why it is the vein most often nicked in a difficult sternotomy.

# Item
## label
The superior vena cava drains the upper half of the body, has the azygos vein as its only tributary, and enters the right atrium behind the third right costal cartilage

## id
CON-CVS-D2307B39C55336

## canonical_key
superior-vena-cava.formation-course-and-tributaries

## definition
The superior vena cava drains venous blood from the head, neck, both upper limbs and the thoracic wall — the upper half of the body. About 5 cm long, it is formed by the union of the two brachiocephalic veins behind the lower border of the first right costal cartilage; its upper half lies in the superior mediastinum and its lower half in the middle mediastinum inside the fibrous pericardium, which it pierces at the level of the second right costal cartilage. It ends by opening into the right atrium behind the third right costal cartilage. Its only tributary is the azygos vein, entering from behind at the second right costal cartilage just before the cava pierces the pericardium.

## explicit_objective
State the two veins that form the superior vena cava, its one named tributary, and the level at which it opens into the right atrium.

## pitfalls
Listing several tributaries for the superior vena cava. The book gives it exactly one — the azygos vein — everything else that drains the upper body reaches it indirectly, through the brachiocephalic veins upstream.

# Item
## label
The phrenic nerve enters the thorax on the venous side (right) or arterial side (left) of the mediastinum and leaves through the diaphragm it alone supplies motor to

## id
CON-RES-AFCED19BA66ED2

## canonical_key
phrenic-nerve.course-and-distribution

## definition
Each phrenic nerve enters the thorax through the thoracic inlet in front of the subclavian artery and behind the start of the brachiocephalic vein, crosses the internal thoracic artery laterally to medially, and descends in front of its lung's root, separated from it by mediastinal pleura, accompanied by the pericardiacophrenic vessels. The right phrenic nerve, shorter because the right cupola is higher and its own course more vertical, runs down the right side of the right brachiocephalic vein, the SVC, the pericardium over the right atrium and the IVC, leaving through the vena caval opening on the IVC's right. The left phrenic nerve runs down the left side of the left common carotid and subclavian arteries, crossing in front of the left vagus, then the aortic arch, crossed by the left superior intercostal vein, then the pericardium over the left ventricle, leaving by piercing the left cupola directly. It supplies the whole of the diaphragm's motor innervation, arising in the neck because the diaphragm itself develops there and descends, pulling the nerve down with it.

## explicit_objective
Contrast the right and left phrenic nerve's medial relations in the thorax and state why the right nerve is shorter than the left.

## pitfalls
Assigning the phrenic nerve's course by symmetry. The right nerve runs down a column of veins (brachiocephalic vein, SVC, IVC); the left runs down a column of arteries (carotid, subclavian, aortic arch) — genuinely different neighbours on the two sides, not a mirror image.

# Item
## label
The right and left vagus nerves take different paths through the superior mediastinum before both break up behind their lung roots into the pulmonary and oesophageal plexuses

## id
CON-CVS-E9CF510CBD01BC

## canonical_key
vagus-nerve.thoracic-course-and-plexuses

## definition
The right vagus nerve enters the thorax crossing in front of the first part of the subclavian artery — where it gives its recurrent laryngeal branch — then descends on the trachea's right side behind the right brachiocephalic vein and upper SVC, crossed on its right by the azygos arch, to reach the back of the right bronchus. The left vagus descends between the left common carotid and subclavian arteries, behind the left brachiocephalic vein, crossed by the left phrenic nerve, then crosses the aortic arch's left anterior aspect — crossed there by the left superior intercostal vein — to reach the back of the left bronchus. Behind each lung root the nerve breaks into the posterior pulmonary plexus (with filaments to the anterior plexus too); below the root, branches form the oesophageal plexuses, right vagus mainly posterior, left mainly anterior; near the diaphragm the two reform as the posterior and anterior gastric nerves respectively.

## explicit_objective
State where each vagus nerve crosses relative to the aortic arch or the subclavian artery, and trace the fate of its fibres from the pulmonary plexus to the gastric nerves.

## pitfalls
Giving both vagi the same relation to the aortic arch. Only the left vagus crosses the arch itself; the right vagus never touches it, running instead alongside the trachea and being crossed by the azygos arch — an asymmetry the left recurrent laryngeal nerve's separate course depends on.

# Item
## label
The left recurrent laryngeal nerve leaves the vagus on the aortic arch and hooks beneath it beside the ligamentum arteriosum before ascending in the tracheo-oesophageal groove

## id
CON-CVS-E9BA4A6C0E8392

## canonical_key
left-recurrent-laryngeal-nerve.origin-and-course

## definition
The left recurrent laryngeal nerve branches from the left vagus while the vagus lies on the aortic arch's left anterior aspect. It hooks below the arch, behind and to the left of the ligamentum arteriosum, then ascends deep to the arch in the left tracheo-oesophageal groove, leaving the superior mediastinum through the thoracic inlet to enter the root of the neck. In the thorax it gives cardiac branches to the deep cardiac plexus and tracheal and oesophageal branches. Because it hooks around a thoracic structure — unlike the right recurrent laryngeal nerve, which hooks around the right subclavian artery in the neck — it can be compressed by an aortic aneurysm or by enlarged tracheobronchial nodes, producing hoarseness or a persistent cough.

## explicit_objective
State the structure the left recurrent laryngeal nerve hooks around and the groove it ascends in, and name the two thoracic causes of its compression.

## pitfalls
Looking for the right recurrent laryngeal nerve's hook inside the thorax. Only the left nerve hooks around a thoracic structure, the ligamentum arteriosum beneath the aortic arch; the right nerve's equivalent hook is around the right subclavian artery, in the neck, not the chest.

# Item
## label
The thoracic sympathetic chain runs on the rib necks above and the vertebral bodies below, giving cardiac and pulmonary branches from its upper ganglia and the three splanchnic nerves from its lower ones

## id
CON-CVS-6BF9AE4B31AD3D

## canonical_key
thoracic-sympathetic-chain.course-and-branches

## definition
The thoracic sympathetic chain, of eleven or twelve ganglia — the first often fused with the inferior cervical ganglion as the stellate ganglion — continues the cervical chain from in front of the first rib's neck, running in the upper thorax in front of the rib necks and in the lower thorax along the vertebral bodies, the right chain lateral to the azygos vein and the left lateral to the two hemiazygos veins, covered throughout by costal pleura, before passing behind the medial arcuate ligament to become the lumbar chain. Each ganglion connects to its spinal nerve by white and grey rami communicantes. The upper five ganglia send vascular and visceral branches to the aortic and oesophageal plexuses, and the second to fourth to the cardiac and pulmonary plexuses. The lower ganglia give the greater splanchnic nerve (fifth to ninth, ending in the coeliac ganglion), the lesser splanchnic nerve (ninth and tenth, ending in the aorticorenal ganglion) and the lowest splanchnic nerve (the last ganglion, ending in the renal plexus).

## explicit_objective
State which vessel lies medial to the sympathetic chain on each side, and name the three splanchnic nerves with the ganglia each arises from.

## pitfalls
Giving the sympathetic chain the same medial relation on both sides. The right chain is lateral to the single azygos vein; the left is lateral to the two hemiazygos veins — a genuine right-left asymmetry that follows directly from the venous asymmetry described in the intercostal-space chapter.

## primary_node_id

# Item
## label
The trachea is a 10-12 cm tube of 16-20 C-shaped cartilage rings, constricted by the thyroid gland above, the brachiocephalic artery on the right and the aortic arch on the left

## id
CON-RES-A6B5E6793B2E5E

## canonical_key
trachea.structure-relations-and-constrictions

## definition
The trachea continues the larynx from the lower border of C6 in the neck to the sternal angle in the thorax, where it divides into the two principal bronchi; it is 10-12 cm long and 1.5-2 cm wide, lying in the midline except at its lower end, where the aortic arch pushes it slightly to the right. It is kept patent by sixteen to twenty C-shaped cartilage rings, their open backs joined by the trachealis muscle to allow the oesophagus to distend during swallowing, and its lowest ring projects up between the two bronchi as the carina. It may be constricted at its upper end by the thyroid gland, in its middle by the brachiocephalic artery on the right, and at its lower end by the aortic arch on the left. Its thoracic part lies with the manubrium and left brachiocephalic vein in front, the oesophagus and left recurrent laryngeal nerve behind, the right vagus, brachiocephalic artery, right brachiocephalic vein, SVC and azygos arch on its right, and the left subclavian and common carotid arteries and the aortic arch on its left.

## explicit_objective
State the vertebral levels at which the trachea begins and bifurcates, and name the three structures that may constrict it along its length.

## pitfalls
Treating the trachea as perfectly midline throughout. Only its upper part is; at its lower end the aortic arch pushes it slightly to the right, which is the anatomical basis for the carina's asymmetric position on imaging.
