# Item
## label
The right principal bronchus is shorter, wider and more vertical than the left, which is why an inhaled foreign body most often lodges on the right

## id
CON-RES-16C7132ECAA7BF

## canonical_key
principal-bronchi.right-vs-left-differences

## definition
The trachea bifurcates at the sternal angle into right and left principal bronchi, each descending into its lung's root. The right bronchus is shorter, about one inch, wider, and more in line with the trachea at 25 degrees from the midline; it divides into three lobar bronchi, its superior (eparterial) bronchus arising outside the hilum, and is supplied by one bronchial artery from the third right posterior intercostal artery, draining to the azygos vein. The left bronchus is longer, about two inches, narrower and more horizontal at 45 degrees from the midline; it divides into only two lobar bronchi on entering the hilum, and is supplied by two bronchial arteries from the descending thoracic aorta, draining to the superior and inferior hemiazygos veins. Both bronchi share the same lymphatic drainage, to the inferior tracheobronchial and bronchopulmonary nodes, and the same autonomic supply, from the vagi and sympathetic chains via the pulmonary plexuses.

## explicit_objective
State the angle each principal bronchus makes with the midline and explain why an inhaled foreign body more often passes to the right.

## pitfalls
Attributing the right bronchus's vulnerability to foreign bodies only to its width. The book gives two reasons together — it is both wider and more in line with the trachea — and a question naming only one is answering half the mechanism.

# Item
## label
The oesophagus enters the thorax as the most posterior structure of the superior mediastinum and has four constrictions, one where the aortic arch and left bronchus each cross it

## id
CON-GIT-4E4EC465826CF2

## canonical_key
oesophagus.thoracic-course-and-constrictions

## definition
The thoracic oesophagus, about 25 cm long overall, enters the thorax through the thoracic inlet between the trachea in front and the vertebral column behind, running as the most posterior structure of the superior mediastinum to the right of the aortic arch; at the sternal angle it passes behind the left principal bronchus into the posterior mediastinum, descending along the descending thoracic aorta's right side, behind the pericardium and the left atrium, to T7, then deviating forwards and left, crossing in front of the aorta, to leave through the diaphragm's oesophageal opening at T10, one inch left of the midline. It shows four constrictions: at its beginning (17 cm from the incisor teeth), where crossed by the aortic arch (22 cm), where crossed by the left principal bronchus (27 cm) and at the diaphragm (42 cm) — the sites where a swallowed foreign body most often lodges and where a corrosive stricture most often forms.

## explicit_objective
Name the oesophagus's four constrictions in order with their distance from the incisor teeth, and state which two are produced by neighbouring thoracic structures crossing it.

## pitfalls
Treating the four oesophageal constrictions as fixed anatomical narrowings of the wall itself. Two of the four — the aortic arch crossing and the left bronchus crossing — are produced by an adjacent structure pressing on the oesophagus, not by the oesophageal wall narrowing on its own.

# Item
## label
The thoracic duct crosses from right to left behind the oesophagus at T5 and drains the whole body except the right upper quadrant into the left brachiocephalic vein

## id
CON-HEM-9123D4493320A7

## canonical_key
thoracic-duct.course-tributaries-and-termination

## definition
The thoracic duct, the largest lymphatic vessel in the body at about 45 cm, is a beaded, valved vessel beginning in the abdomen at the lower border of T12 as the continuation of the cisterna chyli. It enters the thorax through the aortic opening of the diaphragm between the aorta on its left and the azygos vein on its right, ascends in the posterior mediastinum behind the oesophagus's right border, crosses behind the oesophagus from right to left at T5, then ascends in the superior mediastinum behind the oesophagus's left border and the aortic arch, ending at the root of the neck by opening into the start of the left brachiocephalic vein at the junction of the left internal jugular and subclavian veins. In the thorax it receives the posterior intercostal and posterior mediastinal lymph nodes, and at its termination the left jugular, left subclavian and left bronchomediastinal lymph trunks — draining the two lower limbs, the abdomen (except the upper right lobe of liver), and the left side of the thorax, upper limb, head and neck.

## explicit_objective
State the vertebral level and direction of the thoracic duct's crossing behind the oesophagus, and name the field of lymph drainage it serves.

## pitfalls
Assuming the thoracic duct drains the whole body. It drains everywhere except the right side of the head, neck, thorax and upper limb, and the upper surface of the right lobe of the liver — that remaining territory is the right lymphatic duct's alone.

# Item
## label
The right lymphatic duct is a one-centimetre trunk formed by three jugular, subclavian and bronchomediastinal trunks, draining the right side of the head, neck, upper limb, thorax and the liver's right lobe

## id
CON-HEM-D7533F74F06758

## canonical_key
right-lymphatic-duct.formation-and-drainage

## definition
The right lymphatic duct, the thoracic duct's short right-sided counterpart, is only about one centimetre long, lying in the right side of the root of the neck. It is formed by the union of the right jugular lymph trunk, draining the right side of the head and neck, the right subclavian lymph trunk, draining the right upper limb, and the right bronchomediastinal lymph trunk, draining the right side of the thorax and the upper surface of the right lobe of the liver. It ends by opening into the angle between the right internal jugular and right subclavian veins.

## explicit_objective
Name the three lymph trunks that form the right lymphatic duct and state where it opens.

## pitfalls
Giving the right lymphatic duct a long course like the thoracic duct. It is only about a centimetre long — a short confluence in the root of the neck, not a vessel that traverses the thorax.

# Item
## label
Lymph nodes of the thorax fall into six groups by site — parasternal, intercostal, diaphragmatic, brachiocephalic, posterior mediastinal, and the pulmonary/bronchopulmonary/tracheobronchial/paratracheal chain — feeding the bronchomediastinal trunks

## id
CON-HEM-B7A01B9D061793

## canonical_key
lymph-nodes-of-thorax.groups-and-drainage

## definition
Six groups of lymph nodes drain the thorax. Parasternal nodes, alongside the internal thoracic artery in the upper six spaces, drain the deep anterior wall and medial breast. Intercostal nodes, at the posterior ends of the spaces, drain the deep posterolateral wall; with paratracheal and brachiocephalic efferents they form the bronchomediastinal trunk, which opens into the thoracic duct on the left and the right lymphatic duct (or directly into the brachiocephalic veins) on the right. Diaphragmatic nodes — anterior, posterior and two lateral phrenic groups — drain the diaphragm and, anteriorly, the liver's upper right surface. Brachiocephalic (anterior mediastinal) nodes, along the left brachiocephalic vein, drain the pericardium, heart and oesophagus. Posterior mediastinal nodes, near the oesophagus and descending aorta, drain the oesophagus and pericardium and join the thoracic duct. The lung, bronchi and trachea drain through pulmonary nodes within the lung, bronchopulmonary nodes at the hilum, tracheobronchial nodes at the bifurcation, and paratracheal nodes alongside the trachea.

## explicit_objective
Name the six groups of thoracic lymph nodes by site and state which trunk each group's efferents ultimately join.

## pitfalls
Sending every group's efferents to the thoracic duct. Only some do; the intercostal and paratracheal groups feed the bronchomediastinal trunk, which itself splits — left to the thoracic duct, right to the right lymphatic duct or the brachiocephalic vein directly.

# Item
## label
The lung bud arises as an endodermal outgrowth of the foregut in the fourth week, and the tracheoesophageal septum separates it from the oesophagus

## id
CON-DEV-6697214B1AAC0D

## canonical_key
respiratory-diverticulum.origin-and-tracheoesophageal-septum

## definition
The respiratory diverticulum, or lung bud, appears in the fourth week as an outgrowth from the ventral wall of the foregut, its epithelium endodermal — giving the lining of the larynx, trachea, bronchi and lungs — and its cartilage, muscle and connective tissue derived from the surrounding splanchnic mesoderm. Initially it opens freely into the foregut; as it expands caudally, two longitudinal tracheoesophageal ridges appear and fuse to form the tracheoesophageal septum, dividing the foregut into a dorsal oesophagus and a ventral trachea and lung buds, the two remaining connected only through the laryngeal orifice.

## explicit_objective
State the germ layer origin of the respiratory epithelium versus its supporting tissue, and name the structure that separates the trachea from the oesophagus.

## pitfalls
Giving the trachea's cartilage and muscle an endodermal origin along with its lining. Only the epithelium is endodermal, from the foregut lining; the cartilage, muscle and connective tissue come from the splanchnic mesoderm around it.

# Item
## label
The bronchial tree forms by repeated division of the two bronchial buds — three secondary bronchi on the right, two on the left — through seventeen prenatal and six postnatal generations

## id
CON-DEV-FF2A00EA73BDF9

## canonical_key
bronchial-tree.developmental-branching

## definition
As the laryngotracheal tube separates from the foregut it forms the trachea and two bronchial buds, each enlarging into a main bronchus. The right main bronchus branches into three secondary (lobar) bronchi and the left into two, matching the adult lobar pattern; further division produces ten tertiary (segmental) bronchi on the right and eight on the left, establishing the bronchopulmonary segments. Division continues through seventeen generations before birth and six more after birth. The larynx develops separately: its lining is endodermal, from the floor of the pharyngeal gut, while its cartilages and muscles come from the mesenchyme of the fourth and sixth pharyngeal arches, whose rapid proliferation reshapes the laryngeal orifice from a sagittal slit to its adult T-shape, and temporary epithelial occlusion followed by recanalisation produces the laryngeal ventricles.

## explicit_objective
State the number of secondary bronchi on each side and the total generations of airway division completed before and after birth.

## pitfalls
Assuming all airway branching is complete at birth. The book states seventeen generations form before birth and six more afterwards — postnatal branching continues, which is why a newborn's airway tree is not yet the adult's.

# Item
## label
A tracheoesophageal fistula, from incomplete partitioning by the tracheoesophageal septum, is a component of the VACTERL association of birth defects

## id
CON-DEV-01BCF61498D863

## canonical_key
tracheoesophageal-fistula.types-and-vacterl-association

## definition
Oesophageal atresia with or without tracheoesophageal fistula results from an abnormality in how the tracheoesophageal septum partitions the foregut into trachea and oesophagus. It is associated with other birth defects, forming a component of the VACTERL association: vertebral anomalies, anal atresia, cardiac defects, tracheoesophageal fistula, oesophageal atresia, renal anomalies and limb defects. Two other lung anomalies arise separately in this chapter's account: respiratory distress syndrome, from failure of the alveoli to ventilate through absence of surfactant, and congenital cysts of the lung, from dilation of terminal or larger bronchi.

## explicit_objective
Expand the VACTERL acronym and state which single letter stands for tracheoesophageal fistula.

## pitfalls
Treating a tracheoesophageal fistula as an isolated anomaly. The book frames it explicitly as one component of a recognised association of birth defects, VACTERL, so its presence is a prompt to look for the other six.

# Item
## label
The diaphragm forms from four fused mesodermal sources — the septum transversum with cervical myotomes, the two pleuroperitoneal membranes, the body-wall mesoderm and the oesophageal mesentery — and each failure produces a named hernia

## id
CON-DEV-8265C9033E19B1

## canonical_key
diaphragm.embryonic-origin-and-anomalies

## definition
The diaphragm is entirely mesodermal, formed by the union of four sources. The septum transversum, invaded by the third to fifth cervical myotomes and carrying the phrenic nerve down with it as it descends to the lower thoracic cavity, forms the central tendon; it leaves an incomplete septum, the pericardioperitoneal canal, on each side. The two pleuroperitoneal membranes grow medially to close those canals, forming the diaphragm's dorsolateral parts. The body-wall mesoderm grows inwards to form the periphery, and the oesophageal mesentery and the mesoderm around the aorta form the crura. Failure of a small part between the sternal and costal components gives the parasternal hernia of Morgagni; failure of the pleuroperitoneal membranes to close the canals gives the congenital diaphragmatic hernia of Bochdalek; and widening of the oesophageal hiatus with a short oesophagus gives a congenital hiatus hernia.

## explicit_objective
Name the four embryonic sources of the diaphragm and match each of the three named congenital diaphragmatic hernias to the developmental failure that produces it.

## pitfalls
Attributing the Bochdalek hernia to the septum transversum. It is the pleuroperitoneal membranes that fail to close the pericardioperitoneal canal in this anomaly — the septum transversum's own contribution, the central tendon, is a different structure from a different failure, the Morgagni hernia.

# Item
## label
Two fused endocardial tubes form the primitive heart tube, which bends into a C then an S shape as the bulbus cordis moves right and down and the atrium moves up and back

## id
CON-DEV-4E02B436616DC0

## canonical_key
primitive-heart-tube.formation-and-cardiac-looping

## definition
The cardiovascular system is the first major system to function in the embryo, appearing in the middle of the third week. Mesenchymal cells in the cardiogenic area, cranial to the buccopharyngeal membrane, form two endocardial tubes that fuse into a single primitive heart tube, which comes to lie ventral to the foregut once the head fold forms; the tube itself becomes the endocardium, and the surrounding splanchnic mesoderm becomes the myocardium and epicardium. Three constrictions divide it, caudal to cranial after folding, into the sinus venosus, primitive atrium, primitive ventricle and bulbus cordis, with the narrow junction between atrium and ventricle becoming the atrioventricular canal. As the tube elongates it bends first into a C then an S shape: the bulbus cordis moves inferiorly, ventrally and to the right, the primitive ventricle moves ventrally and to the left, and the primitive atrium moves posteriorly and superiorly. If the tube bends left instead of right, the result is dextrocardia, a mirror-image reversal of all the heart's chambers and vessels.

## explicit_objective
Name the four chambers the primitive heart tube divides into from caudal to cranial, and state the direction each moves in as the tube loops.

## pitfalls
Reversing the direction of normal cardiac looping. The bulbus cordis moves right and the atrium moves left-then-back in the normal pattern; a left-ward bend of the tube itself, not a normal-direction error, is what produces dextrocardia.

# Item
## label
The sinus venosus's right horn is absorbed into the right atrium as the sinus venarum, and its left horn shrinks to become the coronary sinus

## id
CON-DEV-13EE046ACBE541

## canonical_key
sinus-venosus.fate

## definition
The sinus venosus, the most caudal chamber of the heart tube, has a small median part and two horns, each receiving a vitelline, an umbilical and a common cardinal vein, and opens into the primitive atrium through a sino-atrial orifice guarded by right and left venous valves. A left-to-right shunt directs most blood into the right horn, which enlarges to receive the SVC and IVC, while the left horn shrinks and is pulled left to become the coronary sinus. After the interatrial septum forms, the right horn and the median part are absorbed into the right atrium as the sinus venarum, its smooth posterior part. The two venous valves fuse cranially into the septum spurium, which joins the interatrial septum on the left; the right valve stretches out and subdivides into the crista terminalis, the valve of the IVC and the valve of the coronary sinus.

## explicit_objective
State what the right and left horns of the sinus venosus each become in the adult heart, and name the three adult derivatives of the right venous valve.

## pitfalls
Giving the coronary sinus a right-horn origin. It comes from the left horn, the smaller one, which shrinks and is pulled across to the left — the right horn's fate is the sinus venarum, a different structure entirely.

# Item
## label
Septum primum, septum secundum and the atrioventricular cushions together partition the primitive atrium, leaving the foramen ovale as the one right-to-left shunt that survives to birth

## id
CON-DEV-67A96150DDAFD9

## canonical_key
interatrial-septum.formation-septum-primum-and-secundum

## definition
The interatrial septum is built from three parts. Ventral and dorsal atrioventricular cushions fuse across the atrioventricular canal to form the septum intermedium, dividing it into right and left canals. Septum primum, a sickle-shaped septum, grows down from the atrial roof towards the septum intermedium; the gap beneath it, the ostium primum, closes as the two fuse, but before it closes the septum's upper part breaks down to open a new gap, the ostium secundum, so a new shunt opens before the old one shuts. Septum secundum then grows down to the right of septum primum, overlapping the ostium secundum and leaving an oblique passage, the foramen ovale, between the free edge of septum primum and the lower margin of septum secundum — allowing right-to-left flow but not the reverse, and closing functionally only after birth.

## explicit_objective
Name the three septa involved in atrial partitioning in the order they act, and state why the foramen ovale permits flow in only one direction before birth.

## pitfalls
Treating septum primum and septum secundum as sealing the same hole. Septum primum's opening is the ostium secundum; septum secundum's opening, offset from it, is the foramen ovale — the valve action of the foramen ovale depends on these two openings not lining up.

# Item
## label
The interventricular septum has a muscular part growing up from the ventricular floor and a membranous part closing the interventricular foramen from two other sources

## id
CON-DEV-87CC6D0D2EF658

## canonical_key
interventricular-septum.formation-muscular-and-membranous-parts

## definition
The muscular part of the interventricular septum is a sickle-shaped median ridge that grows up from the ventricular floor towards the fused atrioventricular cushions, its concave free edge leaving a crescent-shaped interventricular foramen between itself and the cushions. The membranous part closes that foramen and is formed from two sources: an outgrowth of tissue from the ventral atrioventricular cushion, and the lowermost part of the conus septum, formed by the fused right and left conus ridges. Because the membranous part is the smaller, more complex structure built from two converging sources, most ventricular septal defects involve it rather than the thicker muscular part.

## explicit_objective
Name the two sources of the membranous interventricular septum and state which part of the septum most ventricular septal defects involve.

## pitfalls
Assuming the muscular septum, being larger, is where most defects occur. The book states the opposite: because the membranous part is assembled from two separate sources meeting at one point, it is the site of most ventricular septal defects, not the muscular part.

# Item
## label
The bulbus cordis becomes the right ventricle's proximal part, the infundibulum and aortic vestibule from its conus, and the roots of the aorta and pulmonary trunk from its distal truncus, split by a spiralling septum

## id
CON-DEV-FFBA6FA260E484

## canonical_key
bulbus-cordis.parts-and-fate

## definition
The bulbus cordis has three parts. Its proximal part becomes the main part of the right ventricle. Its midportion, the conus cordis, is absorbed into both ventricles as their smooth parts — the infundibulum of the right ventricle and the aortic vestibule of the left — its own conus ridges fusing into a conus septum that divides it accordingly and joins cranially with the aorticopulmonary septum as one conotruncal septum. Its distal part, the truncus arteriosus, is divided by two spiralling truncal ridges — left and right above, anterior and posterior in the middle, right and left below — that fuse into the spiral aorticopulmonary septum, splitting the truncus into the pulmonary trunk and ascending aorta and explaining the adult relation in which the pulmonary trunk lies anterior, then left, then posterior to the ascending aorta as it winds around it.

## explicit_objective
Name the three parts of the bulbus cordis and the adult structure each becomes, and explain why the spiral course of the truncal ridges produces the adult pulmonary trunk-aorta relation.

## pitfalls
Describing the aorticopulmonary septum as a straight partition. Its course is deliberately spiral, and that spiral is the entire reason the adult pulmonary trunk winds around the ascending aorta rather than running parallel to it.

# Item
## label
Of the six pairs of pharyngeal arch arteries, the third forms the carotids, the fourth forms the subclavian on the right and the arch of the aorta on the left, and the sixth's dorsal segment persists on the left as the ductus arteriosus

## id
CON-DEV-3A610A0FB2823C

## canonical_key
aortic-arches.fate-of-the-six-pairs

## definition
The two primitive aortae, continuous with the paired endocardial heart tubes, each split after folding into a ventral aorta, a dorsal aorta and a first aortic arch connecting them through the first pharyngeal arch. The two ventral aortae fuse into the aortic sac, and as each pharyngeal arch develops the sac sends it a branch, giving six paired aortic arch arteries in turn, not all present simultaneously. Their fates diverge by pair: the first and second largely disappear, leaving only the maxillary and the hyoid/stapedial arteries; the third forms the common carotid artery and the proximal internal carotid on each side; the fourth forms part of the right subclavian artery on the right and the middle part of the aortic arch on the left; the fifth disappears completely; and the sixth's ventral segment forms the pulmonary arteries on each side while its dorsal segment disappears on the right but persists on the left as the ductus arteriosus, connecting the left pulmonary artery to the aortic arch.

## explicit_objective
State the adult derivative of the third, fourth and sixth pairs of aortic arch arteries on each side.

## pitfalls
Giving both sides of the sixth arch artery the same fate. Its dorsal segment disappears on the right but persists on the left as the ductus arteriosus — an asymmetric fate that is the direct embryological reason patent ductus arteriosus is always a left-sided anomaly.

# Item
## label
An abnormal right subclavian artery, a right-sided aortic arch and a double aortic arch all result from the dorsal aortae persisting or disappearing on the wrong side

## id
CON-DEV-451A1EDF703FE4

## canonical_key
aortic-arch-anomalies.abnormal-subclavian-double-arch-and-right-sided-arch

## definition
Three arch anomalies follow directly from the normal fate table for the paired dorsal aortae going wrong on one side. An abnormal (retro-oesophageal) right subclavian artery results when the right fourth aortic arch and right dorsal aorta disappear cranial to the seventh intersegmental artery, so the right subclavian artery instead develops from the right seventh intersegmental artery and the distal right dorsal aorta. A right-sided aortic arch results from persistence of the distal right dorsal aorta with degeneration of the distal left dorsal aorta — the reverse of the normal pattern. A double aortic arch results when both the distal right and distal left dorsal aortae persist together, so neither degenerates.

## explicit_objective
State which dorsal aorta segment fails to regress in a right-sided aortic arch, and which two segments both persist in a double aortic arch.

## pitfalls
Treating a right-sided aortic arch and a double aortic arch as the same anomaly with different severity. They are distinct failures — one dorsal aorta persisting while the other degenerates gives a right-sided arch; both persisting together gives a double arch, which can encircle and compress the trachea and oesophagus in a way a simple right-sided arch does not.

# Item
## label
In the fetus, oxygenated blood bypasses the liver by the ductus venosus and the lungs by the foramen ovale and ductus arteriosus, until the first breath reverses the atrial pressure gradient and closes both shunts

## id
CON-DEV-B33D9F68392311

## canonical_key
fetal-circulation.pathway-and-changes-at-birth

## definition
Oxygenated blood from the placenta travels by the left umbilical vein to the liver, through the ductus venosus to the IVC, into the right atrium and across to the left atrium, then to the left ventricle and out the aortic arch, mainly to the head, neck and upper limbs. Deoxygenated blood from the upper body reaches the right atrium by the SVC, passes to the right ventricle and the pulmonary trunk, and — since the lungs are not yet functioning — mostly bypasses them through the ductus arteriosus to the distal aortic arch, descending aorta and umbilical arteries back to the placenta. At birth, respiration expands the lungs and establishes the pulmonary circulation; left atrial pressure rises as pulmonary venous return increases while right atrial pressure falls as placental flow stops, and that reversed pressure gradient presses septum primum against septum secundum, functionally closing the foramen ovale, while the ductus arteriosus closes by contraction of its own muscular wall. The left umbilical vein later fibroses into the ligamentum teres of the liver, the ductus venosus into the ligamentum venosum, the ductus arteriosus into the ligamentum arteriosum, and the distal umbilical arteries into the medial umbilical ligaments; the fossa ovalis and annulus ovalis are the adult remnants of septum primum and septum secundum.

## explicit_objective
Trace fetal blood from the placenta to the aortic arch, name the two shunts that bypass the fetal lungs, and state the pressure change at birth that closes the foramen ovale.

## pitfalls
Explaining closure of the foramen ovale by a structural event at birth rather than a pressure change. It closes because left atrial pressure rises above right atrial pressure once the lungs take over gas exchange — a functional consequence of breathing, which precedes and drives the anatomical apposition of the two septa.

## field_notes
arabicLabel: Teaching at Kasr Alainy is in English and the department book prints no Arabic term for this structure; not transliterated, because a transliteration is not a reviewed term.
arabicAliases: No Arabic label exists for this concept yet, so it can have no Arabic alternates.
microtopicId: The canonical placement and the module-subject path are already finer than any microtopic in the overlay would be.
nanotopicId: The microtopic level is unused here; a nanotopic beneath it would be finer than anything the department book distinguishes.
approvedFileResourceIds: No file resource has been rights-cleared for this concept; the corpus is a private university collection and nothing in it has been cleared for redistribution.
approvedVideoResourceIds: No video resource has been rights-cleared for this concept; the module's corpus holds no video for this chapter.
atomicClaimIds: This lane's scope is limited to concept and article records only (docs/Kasr-Source-Imports/concept/104-CPS-anatomy-concepts.md and article/104-CPS-anatomy.md); authoring the evidence chain (claim/citation/span records) was out of scope this round, so there is no claim ID to point at yet. medical:audit will report this field as missing until an evidence-authoring pass runs against these records.
resourceOccurrenceIds: Read from the department book by hand rather than by the extraction pipeline, so no corpus occurrence record exists to point at.
sourceCandidateIds: The source is known exactly, not a candidate — it is the department anatomy book named on resource_ids.
mergeIds: Nothing has been merged into this concept.
rejectedMergeCandidateIds: The searches for this label returned no near-miss to decide against, so there is nothing to record here for the next author.
conflicts: The department book states this without qualification; nothing was found to record.
uncertainty: Nothing about this concept is genuinely unsettled at undergraduate level; the department book states it without qualification.
lastReviewed: New record; no reviewer has seen it yet.
reviewDue: Set when the first review completes; a due date before a first review is a date nobody agreed to.
exclusionReason: This concept is not excluded; the field is present and empty so that an exclusion later is a change somebody made rather than a field that was always missing.
relatedArticleIds: ART-104-ANA-AORTIC-ARCH-AND-ITS-DEVELOPMENT is the further-reading article this concept's own teaching article (ART-104-ANA-DEV-HEART-OUTFLOW-AND-CIRCULATION) names in its own related_articles; reused here as the concept-level further-reading link rather than picked separately.
